```python
import sys

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, y, d = input().split()
        points.append((int(x), int(y), d))
    
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    def area_at_time(t):
        x_coords = []
        y_coords = []
        
        for x, y, d in points:
            if d == 'R':
                x_coords.append(x + t)
            elif d == 'L':
                x_coords.append(x - t)
            else:
                x_coords.append(x)
                
            if d == 'U':
                y_coords.append(y + t)
            elif d == 'D':
                y_coords.append(y - t)
            else:
                y_coords.append(y)
        
        x_min = min(x_coords)
        x_max = max(x_coords)
        y_min = min(y_coords)
        y_max = max(y_coords)
        
        return (x_max - x_min) * (y_max - y_min)
    
    critical_times = [0.0]
    
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            x1, y1, d1 = points[i]
            x2, y2, d2 = points[j]
            
            # Meeting in x-direction (same x-coordinate)
            if (d1 == 'R' and d2 == 'L') or (d1 == 'L' and d2 == 'R'):
                # x1 + t = x2 - t or x1 - t = x2 + t
                t1 = (x2 - x1) / 2.0
                t2 = (x1 - x2) / 2.0
                if t1 >= 0:
                    critical_times.append(t1)
                if t2 >= 0:
                    critical_times.append(t2)
            elif (d1 == 'R' and d2 == 'R') or (d1 == 'L' and d2 == 'L'):
                # same direction horizontally
                if x1 == x2:
                    # already have t=0
                    pass
            # Meeting in y-direction (same y-coordinate)
            if (d1 == 'U' and d2 == 'D') or (d1 == 'D' and d2 == 'U'):
                t1 = (y1 - y2) / 2.0
                t2 = (y2 - y1) / 2.0
                if t1 >= 0:
                    critical_times.append(t1)
                if t2 >= 0:
                    critical_times.append(t2)
            elif (d1 == 'U' and d2 == 'U') or (d1 == 'D' and d2 == 'D'):
                if y1 == y2:
                    pass
    
    critical_times = sorted(set(critical_times))
    
    min_area = float('inf')
    for t in critical_times:
        area = area_at_time(t)
        if area < min_area:
            min_area = area
    
    print(f"{min_area:.15f}")

solve()

import sys

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    points = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, y, d = input().split()
        points.append((int(x), int(y), d))
    
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    def area_at_time(t):
        x_coords = []
        y_coords = []
        
        for x, y, d in points:
            if d == 'R':
                x_coords.append(x + t)
            elif d == 'L':
                x_coords.append(x - t)
            else:
                x_coords.append(x)
                
            if d == 'U':
                y_coords.append(y + t)
            elif d == 'D':
                y_coords.append(y - t)
            else:
                y_coords.append(y)
        
        x_min = min(x_coords)
        x_max = max(x_coords)
        y_min = min(y_coords)
        y_max = max(y_coords)
        
        return (x_max - x_min) * (y_max - y_min)
    
    critical_times = [0.0]
    
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            x1, y1, d1 = points[i]
            x2, y2, d2 = points[j]
            
            # Meeting in x-direction (same x-coordinate)
            if (d1 == 'R' and d2 == 'L') or (d1 == 'L' and d2 == 'R'):
                # x1 + t = x2 - t or x1 - t = x2 + t
                t1 = (x2 - x1) / 2.0
                t2 = (x1 - x2) / 2.0
                if t1 >= 0:
                    critical_times.append(t1)
                if t2 >= 0:
                    critical_times.append(t2)
            elif (d1 == 'R' and d2 == 'R') or (d1 == 'L' and d2 == 'L'):
                # same direction horizontally
                if x1 == x2:
                    # already have t=0
                    pass
            # Meeting in y-direction (same y-coordinate)
            if (d1 == 'U' and d2 == 'D') or (d1 == 'D' and d2 == 'U'):
                t1 = (y1 - y2) / 2.0
                t2 = (y2 - y1) / 2.0
                if t1 >= 0:
                    critical_times.append(t1)
                if t2 >= 0:
                    critical_times.append(t2)
            elif (d1 == 'U' and d2 == 'U') or (d1 == 'D' and d2 == 'D'):
                if y1 == y2:
                    pass
    
    critical_times = sorted(set(critical_times))
    
    min_area = float('inf')
    for t in critical_times:
        area = area_at_time(t)
        if area < min_area:
            min_area = area
    
    print(f"{min_area:.15f}")

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Gerald plays the following game. He has a checkered field of size n × n cells, where m various cells are banned. Before the game, he has to put a few chips on some border (but not corner) board cells. Then for n - 1 minutes, Gerald every minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell. He moves each chip from its original edge to the opposite edge. Gerald loses in this game in each of the three cases:

  * At least one of the chips at least once fell to the banned cell. 
  * At least once two chips were on the same cell. 
  * At least once two chips swapped in a minute (for example, if you stand two chips on two opposite border cells of a row with even length, this situation happens in the middle of the row). 



In that case he loses and earns 0 points. When nothing like that happened, he wins and earns the number of points equal to the number of chips he managed to put on the board. Help Gerald earn the most points.

Input

The first line contains two space-separated integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 1000, 0 ≤ m ≤ 105) — the size of the field and the number of banned cells. Next m lines each contain two space-separated integers. Specifically, the i-th of these lines contains numbers xi and yi (1 ≤ xi, yi ≤ n) — the coordinates of the i-th banned cell. All given cells are distinct.

Consider the field rows numbered from top to bottom from 1 to n, and the columns — from left to right from 1 to n.

Output

Print a single integer — the maximum points Gerald can earn in this game.

Examples

Input

3 1
2 2


Output

0


Input

3 0


Output

1


Input

4 3
3 1
3 2
3 3


Output

1

Note

In the first test the answer equals zero as we can't put chips into the corner cells.

In the second sample we can place one chip into either cell (1, 2), or cell (3, 2), or cell (2, 1), or cell (2, 3). We cannot place two chips.

In the third sample we can only place one chip into either cell (2, 1), or cell (2, 4).### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.

# YOUR CODE HERE

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. We can only place chips on border cells that are NOT corners (so edges but not corners)
3. Each chip moves every minute to an adjacent cell, going from one border to the opposite border
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips occupy the same cell
   - Two chips swap positions (they pass each other)

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells (non-corner)**: For an n×n grid, the non-corner border cells are:
   - Top edge: (1, 2) to (1, n-1)
   - Bottom edge: (n, 2) to (n, n-1)
   - Left edge: (2, 1) to (n-1, 1)
   - Right edge: (2, n) to (n-1, n)

2. **Movement pattern**: Each chip starts on a border cell and moves toward the opposite border. For example, a chip at (1, j) will move down to (n, j).

3. **Swapping condition**: Two chips swap if they start on opposite sides and meet in the middle. This happens when one chip moves from top to bottom and another moves from bottom to top, and they meet at the same time.

4. **Collision conditions**:
   - Banned cell: any chip cannot go through banned cells
   - Same cell: chips cannot occupy the same cell at any time
   - Swap: chips cannot pass each other

## Analysis of Constraints

For two chips to avoid swapping and avoid collisions:
- They must not be on opposite sides that would cause them to cross
- The path of each chip must be clear (no banned cells)

Let's think about when we can place 2 chips:
- If we place 2 chips on the same row, they'll move in the same direction and won't swap
- If we place 2 chips on different rows/columns, they might cross

However, for chips to avoid swapping, they cannot be positioned such that they would cross each other in the middle.

Actually, let's reconsider: the problem states that chips swap if they pass each other. This happens when chips are on opposite sides and move toward each other.

## Key Insight

The critical constraint is that chips can't swap. So if we have chips on opposite edges, they will inevitably swap unless they're on the same row or column.

However, after more analysis, the main constraints are:
1. No chip can pass through a banned cell
2. No two chips can meet at the same cell
3. No two chips can swap positions

For chips to avoid swapping, they must either:
- Be on the same row or column (so they move in parallel)
- Or, be positioned such that they don't cross paths

But in an n×n grid, if we have two chips on different edges that are opposite to each other, they will eventually meet in the middle.

After reconsidering the examples:

Example 1: 3×3 with (2,2) banned → output 0
Example 2: 3×3 with no bans → output 1
Example 3: 4×4 with (3,1), (3,2), (3,3) banned → output 1

## Strategy

1. First, determine if we can place just one chip (always possible if there are non-banned border cells)
2. Check if we can place two chips without any violation

For two chips to be placed:
- They must not have any path that goes through banned cells
- They must not occupy the same cell at any time
- They must not swap positions

Two chips will swap if they start on opposite edges and move toward each other.

The safest approach is to check if we can place two chips on the same row or column.

Actually, let's think simpler:

For any position where we can place a chip:
- Check if the chip's path (the straight line from start to opposite edge) contains any banned cells
- If no banned cells, we can place a chip there

For two chips:
- If we place two chips, their paths cannot intersect in a way that causes swapping
- But in a grid, chips on opposite edges will inevitably cross

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Read banned cells
banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Check if we can place chips
# Border non-corner cells: top, bottom, left, right excluding corners
# A chip at (r, c) will move in a straight line until it reaches the opposite edge

def can_place_chip(r, c):
    # Check if path from (r,c) to opposite edge is clear
    # Movement: if on top edge, moves down; if on bottom, moves up; etc.
    
    if r == 1:  # top edge
        # moves down to row n
        for i in range(2, n):  # from row 2 to n-1
            if (i, c) in banned:
                return False
    elif r == n:  # bottom edge
        # moves up to row 1
        for i in range(n-1, 1, -1):
            if (i, c) in banned:
                return False
    elif c == 1:  # left edge
        # moves right to column n
        for j in range(2, n):
            if (r, j) in banned:
                return False
    elif c == n:  # right edge
        # moves left to column 1
        for j in range(n-1, 1, -1):
            if (r, j) in banned:
                return False
    return True

# Find all valid starting positions
valid_positions = []
for r in range(1, n+1):
    for c in range(1, n+1):
        # Only consider non-corner border cells
        if (r == 1 or r == n or c == 1 or c == n):
            if r != 1 and r != n and c != 1 and c != n:  # not corner
                continue
            if (r == 1 and 1 < c < n) or (r == n and 1 < c < n) or (c == 1 and 1 < r < n) or (c == n and 1 < r < n):
                if can_place_chip(r, c):
                    valid_positions.append((r, c))

# Now check if we can place multiple chips
# Two chips can coexist without swapping if:
# 1. They don't occupy the same cell at any time
# 2. They don't swap

# For chips on the same row or column, they won't swap since they move in same direction
# For chips on opposite edges, they might swap

# Actually, if we place chips on opposite edges (top and bottom), they will meet and swap
# So we can only place one chip on each pair of opposite edges

# The maximum number of chips we can place is at most 1 if there's a constraint on swapping

# After studying the problem again, I think the answer is:
# - 0 if no valid positions
# - 1 if at least one valid position (since even with 2 valid positions, they might swap)

# But example 2 output is 1, example 3 output is 1

# Let me reconsider: in example 3, why can't we place 2 chips?
# Because the banned cells block paths.

# Actually, looking at it: the answer might be 1 if there's at least one valid position, 0 otherwise.

# But what if we can place two chips on the same row without conflict?

# If two chips are on the same row (both on top edge), they move down in parallel - they never meet or swap.

# So we need to check if we can place multiple chips on the same edge.

# For a given edge, if a chip at (1, c1) and (1, c2) can be placed without path issues, they coexist.

# The path for a chip at (1, c) is the column c from row 1 to row n.

# So if we have chips at (1, c1) and (1, c2), they only collide if they occupy the same cell at the same time, 
# but since they're on the same row and move independently, they never meet at the same cell.

# Actually, they won't meet since they move in the same direction.

# The only constraint is that each must have a clear path.

# So for a row edge, we can place multiple chips as long as each has a clear path.

# Similarly for a column edge.

# But chips on opposite edges WILL swap.

# So the maximum number of chips is:
# - Sum over each edge of the number of valid positions on that edge (since chips on same edge don't conflict)
# - But we can't mix different edges that would cause swapping

# Actually, we can place chips on the same edge (top or bottom) or on the same column (left or right), 
# just not on opposite edges.

# But if we place a chip on top edge and a chip on bottom edge, they will meet and swap.

# So the maximum is the maximum number of chips we can place on a single edge (top, bottom, left, right).

# For each edge, count valid positions.

# For example in 4x4 with banned cells at (3,1), (3,2), (3,3):
# Top edge: (1,2), (1,3) - check if path clear
# For (1,2): path is (2,2), (3,2), (4,2). (3,2) is banned → invalid
# For (1,3): path is (2,3), (3,3), (4,3). (3,3) is banned → invalid
# Bottom edge: (4,2), (4,3) - similar issues
# Left edge: (2,1) - path: (2,2), (3,2), (4,2). (3,2) banned → invalid
# Right edge: (2,4), (3,4), (4,4). (3,4)? not banned → so (2,4) valid

# So only one valid position.

# Therefore the answer is 1.

# Implementation:

top_valid = 0
bottom_valid = 0
left_valid = 0
right_valid = 0

for c in range(2, n):  # columns 2 to n-1
    if (1, c) not in banned:
        # Check path: cells (2,c), (3,c), ..., (n-1,c)
        path_clear = True
        for r in range(2, n):
            if (r, c) in banned:
                path_clear = False
                break
        if path_clear:
            top_valid += 1

for c in range(2, n):
    if (n, c) not in banned:
        path_clear = True
        for r in range(n-1, 1, -1):
            if (r, c) in banned:
                path_clear = False
                break
        if path_clear:
            bottom_valid += 1

for r in range(2, n):  # rows 2 to n-1
    if (r, 1) not in banned:
        path_clear = True
        for c in range(2, n):
            if (r, c) in banned:
                path_clear = False
                break
        if path_clear:
            left_valid += 1

for r in range(2, n):
    if (r, n) not in banned:
        path_clear = True
        for c in range(n-1, 1, -1):
            if (r, c) in banned:
                path_clear = False
                break
        if path_clear:
            right_valid += 1

max_chips = max(top_valid, bottom_valid, left_valid, right_valid)

if max_chips == 0:
    print(0)
else:
    print(max_chips)

Actually, a chip at (r,n) on the right edge moves horizontally to the left edge. It moves from column n to column 1, so it passes through (r, n-1), (r, n-2), ..., (r, 2), (r,1).

So for (2,4): path through rows 2, columns 4→3→2→1: (2,3), (2,2), (2,1)

So right edge at (2,4) is valid.

Similarly, (3,4) is not on the border (it's not corner), but (3,4) is on the right edge, so it would be considered.

Let me fix the path checking:

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

top_valid = 0
bottom_valid = 0
left_valid = 0
right_valid = 0

# Top edge: (1,c) for c in [2, n-1]
for c in range(2, n):
    path_clear = True
    # Path: from (2,c) to (n-1,c) - vertically down
    for r in range(2, n):
        if (r, c) in banned:
            path_clear = False
            break
    if path_clear:
        top_valid += 1

# Bottom edge: (n,c) for c in [2, n-1]
for c in range(2, n):
    path_clear = True
    # Path: from (n-1,c) to (2,c) - vertically up
    for r in range(n-1, 1, -1):
        if (r, c) in banned:
            path_clear = False
            break
    if path_clear:
        bottom_valid += 1

# Left edge: (r,1) for r in [2, n-1]
for r in range(2, n):
    path_clear = True
    # Path: from (r,2) to (r,n-1) - horizontally right
    for c in range(2, n):
        if (r, c) in banned:
            path_clear = False
            break
    if path_clear:
        left_valid += 1

# Right edge: (r,n) for r in [2, n-1]
for r in range(2, n):
    path_clear = True
    # Path: from (r,n-1) to (r,2) - horizontally left
    for c in range(n-1, 1, -1):
        if (r, c) in banned:
            path_clear = False
            break
    if path_clear:
        right_valid += 1

max_chips = max(top_valid, bottom_valid, left_valid, right_valid)

print(max_chips)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Gerald plays the following game. He has a checkered field of size n × n cells, where m various cells are banned. Before the game, he has to put a few chips on some border (but not corner) board cells. Then for n - 1 minutes, Gerald every minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell. He moves each chip from its original edge to the opposite edge. Gerald loses in this game in each of the three cases:

  * At least one of the chips at least once fell to the banned cell. 
  * At least once two chips were on the same cell. 
  * At least once two chips swapped in a minute (for example, if you stand two chips on two opposite border cells of a row with even length, this situation happens in the middle of the row). 



In that case he loses and earns 0 points. When nothing like that happened, he wins and earns the number of points equal to the number of chips he managed to put on the board. Help Gerald earn the most points.

Input

The first line contains two space-separated integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 1000, 0 ≤ m ≤ 105) — the size of the field and the number of banned cells. Next m lines each contain two space-separated integers. Specifically, the i-th of these lines contains numbers xi and yi (1 ≤ xi, yi ≤ n) — the coordinates of the i-th banned cell. All given cells are distinct.

Consider the field rows numbered from top to bottom from 1 to n, and the columns — from left to right from 1 to n.

Output

Print a single integer — the maximum points Gerald can earn in this game.

Examples

Input

3 1
2 2


Output

0


Input

3 0


Output

1


Input

4 3
3 1
3 2
3 3


Output

1

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. Chips must be placed on "border but not corner" cells (so edges but not corners)
3. Each chip moves towards the opposite edge over n-1 minutes
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips occupy the same cell at any time
   - Two chips swap positions (they pass through each other)

## Key Insights

1. **Border but not corner cells**: These are cells where exactly one coordinate is on the edge (1 or n), and the other is not at endpoint. So for a cell (i,j):
   - Top/bottom edge (i=1 or i=n): j must be from 2 to n-1
   - Left/right edge (j=1 or j=n): i must be from 2 to n-1

2. **Movement pattern**: Each chip moves from its starting position toward the opposite edge. For example:
   - A chip at (1,j) moves down to (n,j)
   - A chip at (i,1) moves right to (i,n)

3. **Swapping condition**: Two chips swap when they cross paths. This happens when two chips start on opposite edges and their paths cross.

4. **Position tracking**: At minute k, a chip starting at (1,j) will be at (k,j). A chip starting at (i,1) will be at (i,k).

## Analysis of Constraints

- **No two chips on the same cell**: This means we need to ensure no two chips end up at the same position at any time.
- **No swapping**: This happens when two chips cross paths.

Let me think about the movement more carefully:
- A chip at (1,j) moves to (2,j), (3,j), ..., (n,j)
- A chip at (n,j) moves to (n-1,j), (n-2,j), ..., (1,j)
- A chip at (i,1) moves to (i,2), (i,3), ..., (i,n)
- A chip at (i,n) moves to (i,n-1), (i,n-2), ..., (i,1)

Two chips swap when they pass each other. For example:
- Chip A at (1,j) and chip B at (n,j): they cross at (k,j) if both are at column j at row k
- Chip A at (i,1) and chip B at (i,n): they cross at (i,k) if both are at row i at column k

## Key Insight

The critical observation is that we cannot place two chips that would cross paths. For chips on the same row or column, we need to ensure they don't cross.

However, looking at the examples:
- In a 3×3 grid with no banned cells, answer is 1 (we can place only one chip)
- In a 3×3 grid with banned cell at (2,2), answer is 0

This suggests that even if we could place chips, we might be limited by the movement constraints.

## Solution Approach

After analyzing the problem, I realize that the key constraint is actually about the movement paths:

1. Chips on opposite edges of the same row or column will cross paths
2. We can place at most one chip per "path" that doesn't lead to collision

But looking more carefully at the examples, I think the maximum number of chips is limited by the available border cells that are safe, and the constraint that no two chips can cross paths.

Actually, let me reconsider: the problem states that we lose if chips swap positions. This happens when two chips cross each other's paths.

For chips on the same row: A chip at (i,1) and (i,n) will cross at some point in the middle.
For chips on the same column: A chip at (1,j) and (n,j) will cross at some point in the middle.

So we cannot have chips on both ends of any row or column.

This means:
- For each row, we can have at most one chip (either on left or right edge)
- For each column, we can have at most one chip (either on top or bottom edge)

But we need to check banned cells and ensure chips don't land on them.

Wait, let me think about the movement again. A chip starting at (1,j) will be at (k,j) at minute k. A chip starting at (n,j) will be at (n-k+1,j) at minute k.

They meet if there exists k such that k = n-k+1, so k = (n+1)/2. This only happens when n is odd.

Similarly for columns.

Actually, the problem might be simpler: we just need to check if any border cell is banned, and determine how many chips we can place.

After more analysis of the examples:
- Example 1: 3×3 with (2,2) banned → answer 0
- Example 2: 3×3 with no banned → answer 1  
- Example 3: 4×4 with (3,1),(3,2),(3,3) banned → answer 1

I think the answer is always either 0 or 1 in these cases. The constraint might be that we can't place two chips because they would either cross or land on banned cells.

## Final Insight

The key is that we can place at most one chip. If any border cell (non-corner) is banned, we might still be able to place zero or one chip.

But let me check the examples more carefully.

Actually, rereading the constraint about swapping: two chips swap if they pass through each other. This means we cannot have chips that would cross paths.

Given that, and looking at the examples, I believe the maximum number of chips is 1 when possible, 0 otherwise.

However, I need to check if there are any cases where we can place more than one chip.

After careful thought, I believe the answer is:
- 0 if we cannot place any safe chip (all border non-corner cells are banned)
- 1 if we can place at least one safe chip

But example 3 shows answer 1 with 3 banned cells - so it seems we can place one chip even with some banned cells.

Let me implement a solution that checks if there exists any border non-corner cell that is not banned.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Store banned cells
banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Check if we can place any chip on border but not corner cells
can_place = False

# Top and bottom edges (row 1 or row n), columns 2 to n-1
for j in range(2, n):
    if (1, j) not in banned and (n, j) not in banned:
        # We can place a chip at (1,j) or (n,j)
        can_place = True
    if (1, j) not in banned and (n, j) not in banned:
        # We can place a chip at (1,j) or (n,j)
        can_place = True

# Left and right edges (column 1 or column n), rows 2 to n-1

# Chip at (i,n) goes to (i,n), (i,n-1), ..., (i,1)
# They meet at column k where k = n-k+1, so k = (n+1)/2.

# This happens only when n is odd. When n is even, n-k+1 ≠ k for any k.

# So for odd n, chips on opposite edges of the same row will swap.

# For even n, they will just pass each other without swapping.

# Wait, the problem says "two chips swapped in a minute" - this happens when they exchange positions.

# In the movement pattern, if a chip from left goes right and one from right goes left, they pass each other at the same time, which is considered swapping.

# So for odd n, any two chips on the same row (one at left edge, one at right edge) will swap.

# For even n, they will just pass each other without swapping? Or is this still considered swapping?

# Let me check the example: n=4, even. A chip at (1,2) and (4,2). 
# At minute 1: (1,2) → (2,2), (4,2) → (3,2)
# At minute 2: (1,2) → (3,2), (4,2) → (2,2)
# At minute 3: (1,2) → (4,2), (4,2) → (1,2)

# They swap at minute 2. So even for even n, they swap.

# Actually, at minute k, a chip at (1,j) is at (k+1,j), and a chip at (n,j) is at (n-k+1,j).
# They are at the same position when k+1 = n-k+1, so 2k = n, so k = n/2.

# This happens when n is even.

# So when n is even, k = n/2 is integer, so they swap at minute n/2.
# When n is odd, k = (n+1)/2 is not integer, so they don't meet at the same time.

# Wait, at time t, chip from top at (1,j) is at (t+1,j)
# Chip from bottom at (n,j) is at (n-t+1,j)

# They are at same position when t+1 = n-t+1, so 2t = n, so t = n/2.

# So when n is even, they meet at t = n/2. When n is odd, no integer t satisfies this.

# Therefore:
# - If n is odd: chips on opposite edges of same row do NOT swap (they don't meet at same time)
# - If n is even: chips on opposite edges of same row DO swap (at t = n/2)

# Similarly for columns.

# But the problem says "two chips swapped in a minute" - this happens when they pass each other.

# So for chips on the same row with one at left edge and one at right edge:
# - If n is odd: no swap (safe)
# - If n is even: swap (not allowed)

# For chips on the same column with one at top edge and one at bottom edge:
# - If n is odd: no swap (safe)
# - If n is even: swap (not allowed)

# Also, two chips on the same position at any time is not allowed.

# And landing on banned cells is not allowed.

# So we need to find the maximum number of chips we can place.

# But from examples:
# n=3 (odd): answer 0 for example 1, 1 for example 2
# n=4 (even): answer 1 for example 3

# In example 1 (n=3, m=1, banned at (2,2)): answer 0
# In example 2 (n=3, m=0): answer 1
# In example 3 (n=4, m=3, banned at (3,1),(3,2),(3,3)): answer 1

# Let me check if we can place chips in example 1:
# n=3, banned at (2,2)
# Possible starting positions: (1,2), (2,1), (2,3), (3,2)

# (1,2): moves through (1,2), (2,2), (3,2) - (2,2) is banned → invalid
# (2,1): moves through (2,1), (2,2), (2,3) - (2,2) is banned → invalid  
# (2,3): moves through (2,3), (2,2), (2,1) - (2,2) is banned → invalid
# (3,2): moves through (3,2), (2,2), (1,2) - (2,2) is banned → invalid

# So no valid positions → answer 0

# Example 2 (n=3, no banned):
# (1,2): goes (1,2),(2,2),(3,2) - all valid
# (2,1): goes (2,1),(2,2),(2,3) - all valid
# (2,3): goes (2,3),(2,2),(2,1) - all valid
# (3,2): goes (3,2),(2,2),(1,2) - all valid

# But if we place two chips, they might swap. For example, (1,2) and (3,2):
# At minute 1: (1,2) at (2,2), (3,2) at (2,2) → same cell → invalid
# At minute 1: both at (2,2) → violates "two chips on the same cell"

# So we can only place one chip.

# Example 3 (n=4, banned at (3,1),(3,2),(3,3)):
# Possible positions: (1,2),(1,3), (2,1),(2,4), (3,1) banned, (3,2) banned, (3,3) banned, (4,1),(4,2),(4,3)
# (1,2): goes (1,2),(2,2),(3,2),(4,2) - (3,2) is banned → invalid
# (1,3): goes (1,3),(2,3),(3,3),(4,3) - (3,3) is banned → invalid
# (2,1): goes (2,1),(2,2),(2,3),(2,4) - (2,2),(2,3) not banned, (2,4) not banned → valid
# (2,4): goes (2,4),(2,3),(2,2),(2,1) - all not banned → valid
# (4,1): goes (4,1),(3,1),(2,1),(1,1) - (3,1) banned → invalid
# (4,2): goes (4,2),(3,2),(2,2),(1,2) - (3,2) banned → invalid
# (4,3): goes (4,3),(3,3),(2,3),(1,3) - (3,3) banned → invalid

# So valid positions: (2,1), (2,4)

# But if we place both, do they swap? 
# (2,1) and (2,4): both move in row 2
# At minute 1: (2,1) at (2,2), (2,4) at (2,3)
# At minute 2: (2,1) at (2,3), (2,4) at (2,2)
# At minute 2: they are at (2,3) and (2,2) - different cells
# At minute 3: (2,1) at (2,4), (2,4) at (2,1)

# They swap at minute 3! So we cannot place both.

# So we can place at most one chip.

# Therefore, the answer is: if there exists at least one valid position (where the chip doesn't go through any banned cell), answer is 1, else 0.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Store banned cells
banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Check if any border non-corner cell position is valid
# A position is valid if the entire path doesn't go through a banned cell

# For a chip at (1,j): path is (1,j), (2,j), ..., (n,j)
# For a chip at (i,1): path is (i,1), (i,2), ..., (i,n)
# For a chip at (n,j): path is (n,j), (n-1,j), ..., (1,j)
# For a chip at (i,n): path is (i,n), (i,n-1), ..., (i,1)

valid_positions = []

# Top and bottom edges (row 1 or row n), columns 2 to n-1
for j in range(2, n):
    # Check starting position (1,j)
    valid = True
    for r in range(1, n + 1):
        if (r, j) in banned:
            valid = False
            break
    if valid:
        valid_positions.append((1, j))
    
    # Check starting position (n,j)
    valid = True
    for r in range(n, 0, -1):
        if (r, j) in banned:
            valid = False
            break
    if valid:
        valid_positions.append((n, j))

# Left and right edges (column 1 or column n), rows 2 to n-1
for i in range(2, n):
    # Check starting position (i,1)
    valid = True
    for c in range(1, n + 1):
        if (i, c) in banned:
            valid = False
            break
    if valid:
        valid_positions.append((i, 1))
    
    # Check starting position (i,n)
    valid = True
    for c in range(n, 0, -1):
        if (i, c) in banned:
            valid = False
            break
    if valid:
        valid_positions.append((i, n))

# If we have at least one valid position, we can place one chip
# (we cannot place more than one due to constraints)
if valid_positions:
    print(1)
else:
    print(0)
```

# (2,3): goes (2,3),(2,2),(2,1) - all valid
# (3,2): goes (3,2),(2,2),(1,2) - all valid

# (4,2): goes (4,2),(3,2),(2,2),(1,2) - (3,2) banned → invalid
# (4,3): goes (4,3),(3,3),(2,3),(1,3) - (3,3) banned → invalid

# So valid positions: (2,1), (2,4)

# But if we place both, do they swap? 
# (2,1) and (2,4): both move in row 2
# At minute 1: (2,1) at (2,2), (2,4) at (2,3)

# At minute 3: (2,1) at (2,4), (2,4) at (2,1)

# They swap at minute 3! So we cannot place both.

# So we can place at most one chip.

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Gerald plays the following game. He has a checkered field of size n × n cells, where m various cells are banned. Before the game, he has to put a few chips on some border (but not corner) board cells. Then for n - 1 minutes, Gerald every minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell. He moves each chip from its original edge to the opposite edge. Gerald loses in this game in each of the three cases:

  * At least one of the chips at least once fell to the banned cell. 
  * At least once two chips were on the same cell. 
  * At least once two chips swapped in a minute (for example, if you stand two chips on two opposite border cells of a row with even length, this situation happens in the middle of the row). 



In that case he loses and earns 0 points. When nothing like that happened, he wins and earns the number of points equal to the number of chips he managed to put on the board. Help Gerald earn the most points.

Input

The first line contains two space-separated integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 1000, 0 ≤ m ≤ 105) — the size of the field and the number of banned cells. Next m lines each contain two space-separated integers. Specifically, the i-th of these lines contains numbers xi and yi (1 ≤ xi, yi ≤ n) — the coordinates of the i-th banned cell. All given cells are distinct.

Consider the field rows numbered from top to bottom from 1 to n, and the columns — from left to right from 1 to n.

Output

Print a single integer — the maximum points Gerald can earn in this game.

Examples

Input

2 2


Output

0


Input

3 0


Output

1


Input

3 1

3 3


Output

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. We can only place chips on border cells that are NOT corners (so edges but not corners)
3. For n-1 minutes, each chip moves to an adjacent cell each minute
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips land on the same cell
   - Two chips swap positions (they pass through each other)

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells (not corners)**: For an n×n grid, border cells that are not corners are:
   - Top edge: (1, 2) to (1, n-1)
   - Bottom edge: (n, 2) to (n, n-1) 
   - Left edge: (2, 1) to (n-1, 1)
   - Right edge: (2, n) to (n-1, n)

2. **Movement pattern**: Each chip moves from its starting position toward the opposite edge. If a chip starts at position (r, c), after k minutes it will be at position (r, c+k) or (r, c-k) depending on direction.

3. **Swapping condition**: Two chips swap if they move toward each other and meet exactly in the middle. This happens when they start at symmetric positions relative to the center.

4. **Key observation**: For chips to not swap, they must not be on symmetric positions that would cause them to meet in the middle. Also, they can't be on the same path.

5. **Banned cell constraint**: A chip cannot pass through any banned cell.

## Analysis of the Problem

Let me think about when we can place chips:

- If we place chips on positions that are "safe" from both banned cells and from each other, we can win.
- For two chips to coexist without swapping, they must not be positioned such that they would meet in the middle.
- In a grid, chips on opposite edges can only meet in the middle if they're symmetric.

## Strategy

1. First, identify all valid border cells (non-corner border cells)
2. For each valid border cell, check if a chip can reach the opposite edge without hitting banned cells
3. Determine if we can place multiple chips without conflicts

After analyzing the examples:

- In example 1: n=3, banned at (2,2). Corners are (1,1), (1,3), (3,1), (3,3). Valid border cells are (1,2), (3,2), (2,1), (2,3).

Similarly, chip at (3,2) would pass through (2,2) and fail.

Actually, the problem says "moves each chip from its original edge to the opposite edge". It seems like each chip moves from one edge to the opposite edge.

For a chip at (1,2), it moves vertically from row 1 to row n, through columns 2.
For a chip at (2,1), it moves horizontally from col 1 to col n, through rows 2.

So chip at (1,2) path: (1,2) → (2,2) → (3,2)
Chip at (3,2) path: (3,2) → (2,2) → (1,2)

If we place both, they meet at (2,2) and swap, which violates the swap condition.

So we can only place one chip, or none if any path goes through banned cells.

## Solution Approach

1. For each valid border cell, determine if it can reach the opposite edge without hitting banned cells
2. Count how many such cells we can use
3. But we also need to avoid the swap condition - so chips must not be on paths that would cross in the middle

For bottom row:

The rule seems to be:
1. Find all non-corner border cells where the path to opposite edge doesn't go through any banned cell
2. Among those, we cannot place more than one chip due to swap condition
3. So answer is either 0 (no valid starting positions) or 1 (at least one valid starting position)

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    banned = set()
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        banned.add((x, y))
    
    # Check if we can place any chip
    valid_positions = []
    
    # Top edge: row 1, columns 2 to n-1
    for j in range(2, n):
        if (1, j) not in banned:
            # Check if the path from (1,j) to (n,j) passes through banned cells
            path_valid = True
            for i in range(2, n):
                if (i, j) in banned:
                    path_valid = False
                    break
            if path_valid:
                valid_positions.append((1, j))
    
    # Bottom edge: row n, columns 2 to n-1  
    for j in range(2, n):
        if (n, j) not in banned:
            path_valid = True
            for i in range(1, n-1):
                if (n - i, j) in banned:  # Moving from (n,j) to (1,j)
                    path_valid = False
                    break
            if path_valid:
                valid_positions.append((n, j))
    
    # Left edge: column 1, rows 2 to n-1
    for i in range(2, n):
        if (i, 1) not in banned:
            path_valid = True
            for j in range(2, n):
                if (i, j) in banned:
                    path_valid = False
                    break
            if path_valid:
                valid_positions.append((i, 1))
    
    # Right edge: column n, rows 2 to n-1
    for i in range(2, n):
        if (i, n) not in banned:
            path_valid = True
            for j in range(n-1, 1, -1):
                if (i, j) in banned:
                    path_valid = False
                    break
            if path_valid:
                valid_positions.append((i, n))
    
    # If we have any valid position, we can place at most 1 chip
    # (due to swap condition between any two chips)
    if valid_positions:
        return 1
    else:
        return 0

print(solve())
```

def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    banned = set()
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        banned.add((x, y))
    
    # Check if we can place any chip
    valid_positions = []
    
    # Top edge: row 1, columns 2 to n-1
    for j in range(2, n):
        if (1, j) not in banned:
            # Check if the path from (1,j) to (n,j) passes through banned cells
            path_valid = True
            for i in range(2, n):
                if (i, j) in banned:
                    path_valid = False
                    break
            if path_valid:
                valid_positions.append((1, j))
    
    # Bottom edge: row n, columns 2 to n-1  
    for j in range(2, n):
        if (n, j) not in banned:
            path_valid = True
            for i in range(1, n-1):
                if (n - i, j) in banned:  # Moving from (n,j) to (1,j)
                    path_valid = False
                    break
            if path_valid:
                valid_positions.append((n, j))
    
    # Left edge: column 1, rows 2 to n-1
    for i in range(2, n):
        if (i, 1) not in banned:
            path_valid = True
            for j in range(2, n):
                if (i, j) in banned:
                    path_valid = False
                    break
            if path_valid:
                valid_positions.append((i, 1))
    
    # Right edge: column n, rows 2 to n-1
    for i in range(2, n):
        if (i, n) not in banned:
            path_valid = True
            for j in range(n-1, 1, -1):
                if (i, j) in banned:
                    path_valid = False
                    break
            if path_valid:
                valid_positions.append((i, n))
    
    # If we have any valid position, we can place at most 1 chip
    # (due to swap condition between any two chips)
    if valid_positions:
        return 1
    else:
        return 0

print(solve())

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. We can place chips only on border cells that are NOT corners (so only edge cells excluding corners)
3. Each chip moves toward the opposite edge every minute
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips occupy the same cell at the same time
   - Two chips swap positions (they move past each other)

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells**: For an n×n grid, the border cells are:
   - Top row: (1, j) for j = 2 to n-1
   - Bottom row: (n, j) for j = 2 to n-1  
   - Left column: (i, 1) for i = 2 to n-1
   - Right column: (i, n) for i = 2 to n-1

2. **Movement pattern**: Each chip moves from its starting position toward the opposite edge. For example:
   - A chip at (1, j) moves down to (n, j)
   - A chip at (n, j) moves up to (1, j)
   - A chip at (i, 1) moves right to (i, n)
   - A chip at (i, n) moves left to (i, 1)

3. **Swapping problem**: Two chips swap if they are moving toward each other and cross paths. This happens when:
   - One chip moves from top to bottom and another from bottom to top
   - One chip moves from left to right and another from right to left
   - They cross at the center

4. **Position tracking**: After t minutes, a chip at (i, j) will be at:
   - If starting at top: (t, j)
   - If starting at bottom: (n-t+1, j)
   - If starting at left: (i, t)
   - If starting at right: (i, n-t+1)

5. **Critical observation**: 
   - For a chip to move from one edge to opposite edge without swapping, it must not meet another chip that's moving in the opposite direction.
   - In a row: if we have chips moving from left to right and right to left, they will meet and swap.
   - Similarly for columns.

## Strategy

1. We can only place chips on border edge cells (non-corner)
2. We need to ensure no two chips:
   - Land on banned cells
   - Meet at the same cell
   - Swap positions

   If we place two chips on the same row (both on top or both on bottom), they move vertically toward each other and will swap in the middle - this is not allowed.
   
   Similarly, if we place two chips on the same column (both on left or both on right), they move horizontally toward each other and will swap.

5. Therefore, we can place at most one chip per row and per column on the horizontal edges, and at most one chip per row and per column on the vertical edges.

For example, chips at (1,2) and (3,2): (1,2) moves down, (3,2) moves up. They meet at (2,2) and swap.

So we cannot have two such chips.

Therefore, we can place at most one chip per row and per column in the vertical direction, and at most one chip per row and per column in the horizontal direction.

But actually, we can have:
- One chip moving vertically (top to bottom)
- One chip moving horizontally (left to right)

But if they cross paths, they swap?

The problem says "swapped", not "met".

Upon closer reading: "at least once two chips swapped in a minute". This means they exchanged positions at some time.

If two chips move toward each other and meet, they swap positions.

So if we have a chip at position A moving to B, and another at position C moving to D, and they cross at time t, they swap.

This happens if:
- A chip from top row (1,j) moving down
- A chip from bottom row (n,j) moving up
- They meet at row (t,j) at time t

Or:
- A chip from left column (i,1) moving right
- A chip from right column (i,n) moving left
- They meet at column (i,t) at time t

So we cannot have both a chip moving from top to bottom at column j and a chip moving from bottom to top at column j.

Similarly for left-right in a row.

Therefore, the constraint is: we can have at most one chip moving in each direction per row/column.

But actually, we can have at most one chip per row and per column total, because if we have two chips in the same row, one from top/bottom and one from left/right, they might not swap.

No, if two chips are on the same row: if one is on top and another on bottom, they move toward each other and swap.

Similarly for columns.

So maximum is 1 chip.

Therefore, the answer is:
- If there exists at least one valid edge non-corner position where the path doesn't hit any banned cell, answer is 1
- Otherwise, answer is 0

But wait, what about placing no chips? That would give 0 points, but we want maximum.

If we can place one chip, best is 1 point.

If we can't place any chip, best is 0 points.

So the answer is 1 if there's at least one valid starting position, 0 otherwise.

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=3, banned at (2,2)
- (1,2): at t=1: (2,2) - banned
- (2,1): at t=1: (2,2) - banned  
- (2,3): at t=1: (2,2) - banned
- (3,2): at t=1: (2,2) - banned
All paths hit banned cell → answer 0 ✓

Example 2: n=3, no bans
- (1,2): valid (path (1,2)→(2,2)→(3,2) all valid)
- So answer 1 ✓

Example 3: n=4, banned at (3,1),(3,2),(3,3)
- (1,2): path (1,2)→(2,2)→(3,2)→(4,2). (3,2) is banned → invalid
- (1,3): path (1,3)→(2,3)→(3,3)→(4,3). (3,3) is banned → invalid  
- (2,1): path (2,1)→(2,2)→(2,3)→(2,4). Positions: (2,1),(2,2),(2,3),(2,4). (2,1) to (2,2) to (2,3) to (2,4). Is (3,1) banned? (3,1) is row 3, col 1. (2,1) to (2,2) to (2,3)... no banned cell on path → valid
- (2,4): path (2,4)→(2,3)→(2,2)→(2,1). No banned cells in row 2 → valid
- (3,2): (3,2) is banned - invalid
- (3,3): banned - invalid
- (4,2): (4,2)→(3,2)→(2,2)→(1,2). (3,2) is banned → invalid
- (4,3): (4,3)→(3,3)→(2,3)→(1,3). (3,3) is banned → invalid
- (4,1): (4,1)→(3,1)→(2,1)→(1,1). (3,1) is banned → invalid
- (4,4): (4,4)→(3,4)→(2,4)→(1,4). (3,4) might be valid? But we don't know. The banned cells are only (3,1),(3,2),(3,3). So (3,4) is not banned → valid?

Wait, the banned cells are (3,1),(3,2),(3,3). So (3,4) is valid.

So chip at (4,4): moves to (3,4), (2,4), (1,4). All these are valid? Yes.

So we have valid positions: (2,1), (2,4), (4,4)

But the answer is 1, not 3.

This means my analysis is missing something.

I think I misunderstood the problem. The constraint about swapping applies even if we have only one chip per row/column.

Actually, maybe the answer is always 0 or 1, and if any valid position exists, answer is 1.

In example 3, even though there are multiple valid positions, the maximum points is 1.

Why? Because if we place more than one chip, either:
- They land on banned cells
- They occupy the same cell
- They swap

In example 3 with positions (2,1) and (2,4):
- (2,1) moves right, (2,4) moves left
- They are on the same row
- At t=1: (2,1)→(2,2), (2,4)→(2,3)
- At t=2: (2,1)→(2,3), (2,4)→(2,2)
- At t=3: (2,1)→(2,4), (2,4)→(2,1)

They swap at t=2! So this is not allowed.

Therefore, we cannot place two chips on the same row or same column moving in the horizontal direction, because they will meet and swap.

Similarly, if we place one chip vertically from top and one from bottom in the same column, they will swap.

So we can place at most one chip in each row and each column.

But also, we cannot have two chips in the same row on different edges, as they will swap.

Therefore, maximum is 1 chip.

So the answer is:
- If there exists at least one edge non-corner position where the chip's entire path doesn't hit any banned cell, then answer is 1
- Otherwise, answer is 0

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Read banned cells
banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Check if any edge non-corner position has a clear path
valid_positions = []

# Top row (row 1): columns 2 to n-1
for j in range(2, n):
    # Path: (1,j) -> (2,j) -> ... -> (n,j)
    valid = True
    for t in range(1, n):  # from minute 1 to n-1
        cell = (t + 1, j)  # after t minutes, at row t+1, col j
        if cell in banned:
            valid = False
            break
    if valid:
        valid_positions.append((1, j))

# Bottom row (row n): columns 2 to n-1
for j in range(2, n):
    # Path: (n,j) -> (n-1,j) -> ... -> (1,j)
    valid = True
    for t in range(1, n):
        cell = (n - t + 1, j)
        if cell in banned:
            valid = False
            break
    if valid:
        valid_positions.append((n, j))

# Left column (col 1): rows 2 to n-1
for i in range(2, n):
    # Path: (i,1) -> (i,2) -> ... -> (i,n)
    valid = True
    for t in range(1, n):
        cell = (i, t + 1)
        if cell in banned:
            valid = False
            break
    if valid:
        valid_positions.append((i, 1))

# Right column (col n): rows 2 to n-1
for i in range(2, n):
    # Path: (i,n) -> (i,n-1) -> ... -> (i,1)
    valid = True
    for t in range(1, n):
        cell = (i, n - t + 1)
        if cell in banned:
            valid = False
            break
    if valid:
        valid_positions.append((i, n))

# If we found at least one valid position, answer is 1
# Otherwise, answer is 0
if valid_positions:
    print(1)
else:
    print(0)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Gerald plays the following game. He has a checkered field of size n × n cells, where m various cells are banned. Before the game, he has to put a few chips on some border (but not corner) board cells. Then for n - 1 minutes, Gerald every minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell. He moves each chip from its original edge to the opposite edge. Gerald loses in this game in each of the three cases:

  * At least one of the chips at least once fell to the banned cell. 
  * At least once two chips were on the same cell. 
  * At least once two chips swapped in a minute (for example, if you stand two chips on two opposite border cells of a row with even length, this situation happens in the middle of the row). 



In that case he loses and earns 0 points. When nothing like that happened, he wins and earns the number of points equal to the number of chips he managed to put on the board. Help Gerald earn the most points.

Input

The first line contains two space-separated integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 1000, 0 ≤ m ≤ 105) — the size of the field and the number of banned cells. Next m lines each contain two space-separated integers. Specifically, the i-th of these lines contains numbers xi and yi (1 ≤ xi, yi ≤ n) — the coordinates of the i-th banned cell. All given cells are distinct.

Consider the field rows numbered from top to bottom from 1 to n, and the columns — from left to right from 1 to n.

Output

Print a single integer — the maximum points Gerald can earn in this game.

Examples

Input

3 1
2 2


Output

0


Input

3 0


Output

1


Input

4 3
3 1
3 2
3 3


Output

1

Note

In the first test the answer equals zero as we can't put chips into the corner cells.

In the second sample we can place one chip into either cell (1, 2), or cell (3, 2), or cell (2, 1), or cell (2, 3). We cannot place two chips.

In the third sample we can only place one chip into either cell (2, 1), or cell (2, 4).### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. We can place chips on border cells (but not corners)
3. Each chip moves every minute to an adjacent cell, moving from one border to the opposite border
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip falls on a banned cell
   - Two chips occupy the same cell
   - Two chips swap positions (which happens when they meet at the same point at the same time)

## Key Observations

1. **Border cells (not corners)**: For an n×n grid, border cells are:
   - Top row: (1, 2) to (1, n-1)
   - Bottom row: (n, 2) to (n, n-1)  
   - Left column: (2, 1) to (n-1, 1)
   - Right column: (2, n) to (n-1, n)

2. **Movement pattern**: Each chip moves one cell per minute toward the opposite border. After n-1 minutes, it reaches the opposite border.

3. **Swapping condition**: Two chips swap if they start at symmetric positions relative to the center and meet in the middle. This happens when their paths cross at the same time.

4. **Collision condition**: Two chips occupy the same cell at the same time.

5. **Critical insight**: For chips to move without swapping or colliding, they must not meet at the same point at the same time. This means no two chips can have paths that intersect at the same time.

## Analysis of Constraints

- The only way two chips can meet is if they are on symmetric paths (e.g., one on top-left, one on bottom-right).
- For chips to avoid swapping, they must not be on paths that cross at the same time.
- The problem becomes finding the maximum number of non-interfering chips we can place.

## Key Insight from Examples

Looking at the examples:
- Example 1: n=3, banned cell at (2,2). We can't place any chips (corners not allowed, and the center is banned).
- Example 2: n=3, no banned cells. We can place 1 chip (not 2).
- Example 3: n=4, banned cells at (3,1), (3,2), (3,3). We can only place 1 chip.

The pattern suggests that we can place at most one chip if there are any obstacles, or possibly two chips if they don't interfere.

## Solution Approach

The key insight is that we need to avoid:
1. Chips falling on banned cells
2. Chips meeting on the same cell at the same time
3. Chips swapping positions

For two chips to avoid swapping and meeting, they need to have distinct paths. In a grid, two chips on opposite edges will meet at some point if their paths cross.

Actually, let's reconsider: chips moving from border to opposite border will follow a path. Two chips start at different positions and move toward opposite borders. They will only meet if their paths cross at the same time.

Since all chips move at the same speed, two chips starting at positions that are "opposite" in some sense will meet at the center.

The crucial observation is that we can place at most one chip if there are any constraints. But looking at example 2, we can place 1 chip.

Wait, let me think again. In example 2 (n=3, no banned cells), we can place one chip. In example 3 (n=4, banned cells on row 3), we can only place one chip.

I think the maximum number of chips we can place is 1 in general, because:
- Any two chips on the border will eventually meet or swap at some point
- Moving at constant speed, the paths will cross
- The only safe configuration is to place at most one chip

But let's verify: is it possible to place more than one chip without violating the rules?

For two chips to avoid meeting, they would need to start at positions where they don't cross paths. However, with the movement pattern described (moving from edge to opposite edge), all chips follow a predictable path, and they will inevitably meet or cross at some point.

Therefore, the maximum number of chips is 1, unless we can place 0 chips due to banned positions.

But in example 2, we can place 1 chip. In example 1, we can't place any chips due to the banned cell.

So the algorithm:
1. Check if any border cell (non-corner) is banned
2. If yes, we might need to skip some cells
3. Since two chips will always interfere, we can place at most 1 chip
4. We can place 1 chip if there exists at least one available border cell (non-corner)

Wait, example 3 has banned cells at (3,1), (3,2), (3,3) - these are on the bottom row, which we can't place on anyway since we only place on border cells but not corners.

Actually, let me recheck: the border cells we can use are top, bottom, left, right, but excluding corners.

For n=4:
- Top: (1,2), (1,3)
- Bottom: (4,2), (4,3)  
- Left: (2,1), (3,1)
- Right: (2,4), (3,4)

The banned cells are (3,1), (3,2), (3,3) - so (3,1) is banned on left border. But (3,4) is not banned.

So we can place a chip at (2,1)? (3,1) is banned, so (2,1) is not banned? Wait, the problem says "banned cells", so if (3,1) is banned, that means cell (3,1) is forbidden, not the entire column.

I think I misunderstood. The banned cells are specific cells. If (3,1) is banned, then we cannot place a chip at (3,1).

In the left border, we can place at (2,1) or (3,1). If (3,1) is banned, we can still place at (2,1).

So the answer is 1 because we can place one chip on an available border cell.

## Final Algorithm

1. Identify all available border cells (non-corner)
2. Check if any of these cells are banned
3. If no available border cell, answer is 0
4. Otherwise, answer is 1

But wait - can we place more than one chip without interference? Based on the problem and examples, it seems not. The ordering constraint and swapping condition suggest that two chips will either meet or swap.

Therefore, the maximum points is:
- 0 if no valid border positions exist
- 1 otherwise

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

banned = set()
for _ in range(m):
    x, y = map(int, input().split())
    banned.add((x, y))

# Check available border cells (non-corner)
available = False

# Top row (row 1): columns 2 to n-1
for j in range(2, n):
    if (1, j) not in banned:
        available = True
        break

# Bottom row (row n): columns 2 to n-1
for j in range(2, n):
    if (n, j) not in banned:
        available = True
        break

# Left column (column 1): rows 2 to n-1
for i in range(2, n):
    if (i, 1) not in banned:
        available = True
        break

# Right column (column n): rows 2 to n-1
for i in range(2, n):
    if (i, n) not in banned:
        available = True
        break

if available:
    print(1)
else:
    print(0)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. Chips must be placed on border cells (but not corners)
3. Each chip moves from its starting position to the opposite edge over n-1 minutes
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip reaches a banned cell
   - Two chips occupy the same cell at any time
   - Two chips swap positions (i.e., they pass through each other)
5. We want to maximize the number of chips we can place

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells (not corners)**: For an n×n grid, border cells are those on edges but not corners. For each edge:
   - Top row: cells (1, 2) to (1, n-1)
   - Bottom row: cells (n, 2) to (n, n-1)
   - Left column: cells (2, 1) to (n-1, 1)
   - Right column: cells (2, n) to (n-1, n)

2. **Chip movement**: A chip at position (r, c) will move to position (r', c') after k minutes, where:
   - For vertical movement: from row r to row (n+1-r) or similar pattern
   - After k minutes, a chip starting at (r, c) will be at (r + k - (n-1), c) or similar

3. **Swapping condition**: Two chips swap if they start on opposite edges and meet in the middle. This happens when their paths cross.

4. **Path conflicts**: We need to check if two chips will end up on the same cell or swap positions.

## Analysis of Constraints

- **No two chips on same cell**: This means their paths must never intersect
- **No swapping**: This means if two chips move towards each other, they cross paths

The key insight is that we can only place chips on the border, and we need to avoid:
1. Any chip landing on a banned cell
2. Two chips meeting at the same position at the same time
3. Two chips swapping

## Strategy

Let me think about this more systematically:

For a chip starting at (r, c) on the border:
- After k minutes, its position depends on its starting point and direction
- For simplicity, assume each chip moves directly from its starting position to the opposite edge

The critical observation: if we place two chips on opposite edges, they will meet at some point and either clash or swap.

Actually, let me reconsider. Looking at the examples:

Example 1: n=3, m=1, banned at (2,2). We can't place any chips because borders are (1,2), (2,1), (2,3), (3,2). (2,2) is banned, and if we place a chip at (1,2), it moves to (3,2) - which is banned. Similarly for others.

Example 2: n=3, m=0. We can place one chip on (1,2), (3,2), (2,1), or (2,3). But not two, because they would meet or swap.

Example 3: n=4, banned at (3,1), (3,2), (3,3). We can place one chip at (2,1) or (2,4) - these are safe.

## Key Insight

After analyzing the constraints, I realize that:
- We can place at most one chip on each "path" due to the swapping constraint
- Actually, looking at the problem again, the swapping constraint means that two chips starting on the same horizontal line or vertical line will collide or swap

But the critical insight from the examples is that in many cases, we can only place one chip.

Wait, let me reconsider. The problem seems to be that we can't have multiple chips because their paths will intersect.

Actually, let me think about it differently:

1. Chips are on border but not corners
2. Each chip moves from its starting position to the opposite edge
3. The paths will intersect 

The answer in all examples is either 0 or 1. This suggests we can at most place 1 chip.

But let's verify:
- If n=4 and no banned cells, can we place 2 chips?
- If we place at (1,2) and (1,3): both start on top edge, will they meet? Yes, at some point.
- If we place at (1,2) and (3,2): one starts on top, one on bottom, they move toward each other and will swap.

So in general, any two chips will either:
- Meet at some point (same cell)
- Swap positions (due to path crossing)

Therefore, we can place at most one chip.

But we need to check if any valid position exists.

## Algorithm

1. Find all valid border cells (not corners) that are not banned
2. Check if any such cell is valid (meaning the chip will not reach a banned cell during its journey)
3. Return 1 if at least one valid position exists, otherwise 0

Wait, let me be more careful about the movement.

For a chip at (1, c) on top row (c from 2 to n-1), after k minutes it will be at (1+k, c) or (1-k, c)? 

Actually, if it moves from top to bottom, its position after k minutes might be (1+k, c) until it reaches row n.

Similarly, for a chip at (r, 1) on left column, it moves to (r, 1+k).

The exact path depends on the direction, but the key is that chips on the same row or column will meet or swap.

Since any two chips on the same row or column will likely conflict, and chips on opposite edges will either meet or swap, we conclude:

**We can place at most one chip.**

Therefore:
- If there's at least one valid border cell (not corner) that doesn't have a path to a banned cell, answer is 1
- Otherwise, answer is 0

But what about the path to banned cells? For example, a chip at (1,2) on top row will move down to (n,2). If (n,2) is banned, that's bad. But the movement is continuous - the chip passes through all cells from (1,2) to (n,2).

Actually, the problem says "fell to the banned cell", so if any intermediate cell is banned, we lose.

But the movement seems to be direct - from start to opposite edge.

Wait, let me re-read: "each minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell". So it moves step by step.

So a chip at (1,2) will move to (2,2), then (3,2), then (4,2), etc.

So if any cell on its path is banned, it loses.

For a chip at (1,c) (top edge), its path is (1,c) → (2,c) → (3,c) → ... → (n,c)
For a chip at (r,1) (left edge), its path is (r,1) → (r,2) → (r,3) → ... → (r,n)

The chip path goes from start to opposite edge, passing through all cells in a straight line.

Therefore:

1. A chip at (1,c) will pass through all cells (i,c) for i from 1 to n
2. A chip at (r,1) will pass through all cells (r,j) for j from 1 to n

So the chip fails if any of these cells is banned.

## Final Algorithm

1. For each valid border cell (not corner), check if the entire path to the opposite edge contains any banned cell
2. If at least one such cell exists, answer is 1
3. Otherwise, answer is 0

But wait - what about the swapping constraint? Even if no cells are banned, two chips might swap.

However, since we're only placing one chip, we don't have to worry about swapping.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

if m == 0:
    # No banned cells, so any border cell (not corner) is valid
    # For n >= 3, there are border cells (not corners)
    # For n = 2, border cells are not corners? In 2x2, corners are (1,1), (1,2), (2,1), (2,2)
    # So no border cells (not corner) exist
    if n == 2:
        print(0)
    else:
        print(1)
else:
    banned = set()
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        banned.add((x, y))
    
    # Check if any valid border cell (not corner) has a path that avoids banned cells
    # Valid border cells (not corners):
    # Top row: (1, c) where 2 <= c <= n-1
    # Bottom row: (n, c) where 2 <= c <= n-1  
    # Left column: (r, 1) where 2 <= r <= n-1
    # Right column: (r, n) where 2 <= r <= n-1
    
    found_valid = False
    
    # Check top row
    for c in range(2, n):
        # Path: (1,c) -> (2,c) -> ... -> (n,c)
        valid_path = True
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            if (i, c) in banned:
                valid_path = False
                break
        if valid_path:
            found_valid = True
            break
    
    # Check bottom row
    if not found_valid:
        for c in range(2, n):
            # Path: (n,c) -> (n-1,c) -> ... -> (1,c)
            # But if we go from (n,c) to (1,c), it's the same cells (i,c) for i from 1 to n
            valid_path = True
            for i in range(1, n + 1):
                if (i, c) in banned:
                    valid_path = False
                    break
            if valid_path:
                found_valid = True
                break
    
    # Check left column
    if not found_valid:
        for r in range(2, n):
            # Path: (r,1) -> (r,2) -> ... -> (r,n)
            valid_path = True
            for c in range(1, n + 1):
                if (r, c) in banned:
                    valid_path = False
                    break
            if valid_path:
                found_valid = True
                break
    
    # Check right column
    if not found_valid:
        for r in range(2, n):
            # Path: (r,n) -> (r,n-1) -> ... -> (r,1)
            # Path cells are (r,c) for c from 1 to n
            valid_path = True
            for c in range(1, n + 1):
                if (r, c) in banned:
                    valid_path = False
                    break
            if valid_path:
                found_valid = True
                break
    
    print(1 if found_valid else 0)
```

Wait, I need to be more careful. For a chip at (r,1), it moves to the opposite edge. The path is from (r,1) to (r,n), so it passes through (r,1), (r,2), ..., (r,n).

Similarly for other positions.

But I'm checking the same cells multiple times. I can just check if any valid path exists.

Also, for n=2: border cells (not corners) - there are none. So we should return 0.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Gerald plays the following game. He has a checkered field of size n × n cells, where m various cells are banned. Before the game, he has to put a few chips on some border (but not corner) board cells. Then for n - 1 minutes, Gerald every minute moves each chip into an adjacent cell. He moves each chip from its original edge to the opposite edge. Gerald loses in this game in each of the three cases:

  * At least one of the chips at least once fell to the banned cell. 
  * At least once two chips were on the same cell. 
  * At least once two chips swapped in a minute (for example, if you stand two chips on two opposite border cells of a row with even length, this situation happens in the middle of the row). 



In that case he loses and earns 0 points. When nothing like that happened, he wins and earns the number of points equal to the number of chips he managed to put on the board. Help Gerald earn the most points.

Input

The first line contains two space-separated integers n and m (2 ≤ n ≤ 1000, 0 ≤ m ≤ 105) — the size of the field and the number of banned cells. Next m lines each contain two space-separated integers. Specifically, the i-th of these lines contains numbers xi and yi (1 ≤ xi, yi ≤ n) — the coordinates of the i-th banned cell. All given cells are distinct.

Consider the field rows numbered from top to bottom from 1 to n, and the columns — from left to right from 1 to n.

Output

Print a single integer — the maximum points Gerald can earn in this game.

Examples

Input

3 1
2 2


Output

0


Input

3 0


Output

1


Input

4 3
3 1
3 2
3 3


Output

1

Note

In the first test the answer equals zero as we can't put chips into the corner cells.

In the second sample we can place one chip into either cell (1, 2), or cell (3, 2), or cell (2, 1), or cell (2, 3). We cannot place two chips.

In the third sample we can only place one chip into either cell (2, 1), or cell (2, 4).### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. We can only place chips on border cells that are NOT corners (so edges but not corners)
3. Each chip moves from its initial position toward the opposite edge, one step per minute
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips occupy the same cell at the same time
   - Two chips swap positions (cross paths)

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells (non-corner)**: For an n×n grid, border cells that are not corners are:
   - Top edge: (1,2), (1,3), ..., (1,n-1)
   - Bottom edge: (n,2), (n,3), ..., (n,n-1)
   - Left edge: (2,1), (3,1), ..., (n-1,1)
   - Right edge: (2,n), (3,n), ..., (n-1,n)

2. **Movement pattern**: Each chip moves from its initial position to the opposite edge. For example:
   - A chip at (1,j) moves down to (n,j)
   - A chip at (i,1) moves right to (i,n)
   - A chip at (i,n) moves left to (i,1)

3. **When do chips swap?**: Two chips swap when they cross paths. This happens when:
   - One chip moves horizontally and another moves vertically
   - They meet at some intermediate point

4. **When do chips occupy the same cell?**: This happens when two chips reach the same position at the same time.

5. **When do chips fall on banned cells?**: We need to check if any chip's path passes through banned cells.

## Analysis

Let me think about when we can place k chips:

**Case 1: k = 0** - always possible, but we want maximum

**Case 2: k = 1** - always possible if there's at least one valid starting position

**Case 3: k ≥ 2** - we need to check if we can place multiple chips without violations.

For two chips to not swap or collide:
- They must have different movement paths
- They must not meet at the same cell at the same time
- No chip should pass through a banned cell

For chips moving horizontally vs vertically:
- A chip at (1,j) moving down and a chip at (i,1) moving right will meet at (min(i,n), min(j,n)) if they reach simultaneously
- The meeting happens at time t where t = j-1 and t = i-1, so when j-1 = i-1, i.e., i = j

So chips at (1,j) and (i,1) won't meet if i ≠ j.

But wait - let me reconsider the swapping condition. The problem says "two chips swapped" which means they pass each other exactly once.

Actually, the key insight is that for most configurations, we can place at most one chip. Let me think about this more carefully.

After analyzing the examples:
- Example 1: n=3, m=1 at (2,2) - answer is 0
- Example 2: n=3, m=0 - answer is 1  
- Example 3: n=4, m=3 at (3,1),(3,2),(3,3) - answer is 1

It seems like we can never place more than 1 chip in most cases, and we need to check if we can place any chip at all.

## Solution Strategy

1. First, check if we can place any chip at all (no path goes through banned cells)
2. If we can place at least one chip, answer is 1 (since placing 2 chips seems risky due to collision/swapping)
3. But we need to check if placing one chip is possible

Wait, let me think again. In example 2, n=3, m=0, we can place one chip. In example 3, we can place one chip (on (2,1) or (2,4)).

The constraint about swapping seems particularly problematic for multiple chips. In fact, it seems very difficult to place multiple chips without them swapping or colliding.

After further analysis, I believe the answer is:
- If no valid starting position exists (all border non-corner cells lead to banned cells), answer is 0
- Otherwise, answer is 1

But let me verify with the examples:
- Example 1: (2,2) is banned. Let's check each border non-corner:
  - Top: (1,2) → moves to (3,2). Does (3,2) have a banned cell? No. (2,2) is banned, but that's not on path from (1,2) to (3,2) in the middle? Wait: from (1,2) to (3,2), it passes through (2,2) at time 1. So yes, it falls on banned cell.
  - Bottom: (3,2) → passes through (2,2) at time 1 → banned
  - Left: (2,1) → moves to (2,3) → path is (2,1)→(2,2)→(2,3). At time 1: (2,2) → banned
  - Right: (2,3) → moves to (2,1) → passes through (2,2) at time 1 → banned
  So no valid starting position → answer 0 ✓

- Example 2: no banned cells, so every border non-corner position is valid → answer 1 ✓

- Example 3: banned cells at (3,1),(3,2),(3,3)
  - Top: (1,2) → path: (1,2)→(2,2)→(3,2). (3,2) is banned → invalid
  - Top: (1,3) → path: (1,3)→(2,3)→(3,3). (3,3) is banned → invalid  
  - Bottom: (4,2) → path: (4,2)→(3,2) → (3,2) banned → invalid
  - Bottom: (4,3) → path: (4,3)→(3,3) → banned → invalid
  - Left: (2,1) → path: (2,1)→(3,1) → (3,1) banned → invalid
  - Right: (2,4) → path: (2,4)→(3,4) → (3,4) is not banned → valid!
  So we can place one chip at (2,4) → answer 1 ✓

## Algorithm

1. Identify all border non-corner cells (edges but not corners)
2. For each such cell, simulate the path to check if it passes through any banned cell
3. If any valid starting position exists, return 1
4. Otherwise, return 0

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    banned = set()
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        banned.add((x, y))
    
    # Check all border non-corner cells
    valid_positions = []
    
    # Top edge (row 1): columns 2 to n-1
    for j in range(2, n):
        valid_positions.append((1, j))
    
    # Bottom edge (row n): columns 2 to n-1
    for j in range(2, n):
        valid_positions.append((n, j))
    
    # Left edge (column 1): rows 2 to n-1
    for i in range(2, n):
        valid_positions.append((i, 1))
    
    # Right edge (column n): rows 2 to n-1
    for i in range(2, n):
        valid_positions.append((i, n))
    
    # Check if any position has a valid path (doesn't hit banned cells)
    for (row, col) in valid_positions:
        # Simulate the path - chips move toward opposite edge
        # For top row: moves down to row n
        # For bottom row: moves up to row 1
        # For left col: moves right to col n  
        # For right col: moves left to col 1
        
        if row == 1:  # top edge, moving down
            # Path: (1,col) -> (2,col) -> ... -> (n,col)
            # Check if any intermediate cell is banned
            for r in range(2, n):  # from row 2 to n-1
                if (r, col) in banned:
                    break
            else:
                # No banned cell found on path
                return 1
                
        elif row == n:  # bottom edge, moving up
            for r in range(n-1, 1, -1):  # from row n-1 to 2
                if (r, col) in banned:
                    break
            else:
                return 1
                
        elif col == 1:  # left edge, moving right
            for c in range(2, n):  # from col 2 to n-1
                if (row, c) in banned:
                    break
            else:
                return 1
                
        elif col == n:  # right edge, moving left
            for c in range(n-1, 1, -1):  # from col n-1 to 2
                if (row, c) in banned:
                    break
            else:
                return 1
    
    # No valid position found
    return 0

print(solve())
```
