def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    banned = set()
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        banned.add((x, y))
    
    # Check all border non-corner cells
    valid_positions = []
    
    # Top edge (row 1): columns 2 to n-1
    for j in range(2, n):
        valid_positions.append((1, j))
    
    # Bottom edge (row n): columns 2 to n-1
    for j in range(2, n):
        valid_positions.append((n, j))
    
    # Left edge (column 1): rows 2 to n-1
    for i in range(2, n):
        valid_positions.append((i, 1))
    
    # Right edge (column n): rows 2 to n-1
    for i in range(2, n):
        valid_positions.append((i, n))
    
    # Check if any position has a valid path (doesn't hit banned cells)
    for (row, col) in valid_positions:
        # Simulate the path - chips move toward opposite edge
        # For top row: moves down to row n
        # For bottom row: moves up to row 1
        # For left col: moves right to col n  
        # For right col: moves left to col 1
        
        if row == 1:  # top edge, moving down
            # Path: (1,col) -> (2,col) -> ... -> (n,col)
            # Check if any intermediate cell is banned
            for r in range(2, n):  # from row 2 to n-1
                if (r, col) in banned:
                    break
            else:
                # No banned cell found on path
                return 1
                
        elif row == n:  # bottom edge, moving up
            for r in range(n-1, 1, -1):  # from row n-1 to 2
                if (r, col) in banned:
                    break
            else:
                return 1
                
        elif col == 1:  # left edge, moving right
            for c in range(2, n):  # from col 2 to n-1
                if (row, c) in banned:
                    break
            else:
                return 1
                
        elif col == n:  # right edge, moving left
            for c in range(n-1, 1, -1):  # from col n-1 to 2
                if (row, c) in banned:
                    break
            else:
                return 1
    
    # No valid position found
    return 0

print(solve())

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n grid with m banned cells
2. We can place chips only on border cells (but not corners)
3. Each chip moves every minute to an adjacent cell (up, down, left, right)
4. We lose if:
   - Any chip lands on a banned cell
   - Two chips are on the same cell at the same time
   - Two chips swap positions (they occupy each other's positions)
5. We want to maximize the number of chips we can place

## Key Insights

1. **Border cells (not corners)**: These are edge cells excluding corners. For an n×n grid, border cells are:
   - Top row: (1,2), (1,3), ..., (1,n-1) 
   - Bottom row: (n,2), (n,3), ..., (n,n-1)
   - Left column: (2,1), (3,1), ..., (n-1,1)
   - Right column: (2,n), (3,n), ..., (n-1,n)

2. **Movement pattern**: Each chip starts on a border cell and moves toward the opposite border. After t minutes, a chip at position (r,c) will be at position that depends on its direction.

3. **Swapping condition**: Two chips swap if they start at positions that are symmetric with respect to the center of the grid and move toward each other.

4. **Key observation**: Since chips move every minute and we have n-1 minutes total, after n-1 minutes each chip will have moved to the opposite border. The movement follows a predictable pattern.

5. **Battlefield constraint**: Two chips cannot occupy the same cell at any time, and they cannot swap positions.

## Critical Analysis

The problem is essentially about finding the maximum number of non-conflicting starting positions on the border (excluding corners) such that:
- Each chip's path doesn't hit banned cells
- No two chips collide or swap

Let me think about when chips swap: if two chips start at positions (r1,c1) and (r2,c2) and their paths cross such that they exchange positions, that's a loss.

Actually, let me reconsider. Two chips swap when they start at positions that are symmetric with respect to the center of the grid and move toward each other.

For a chip to move from one border to the opposite border, it will follow a path. The critical insight is that if we place chips at positions that are symmetric across the center, they might swap.

But let me think simpler: we need to avoid any occupation of banned cells, and avoid collisions or swaps.

## Optimal Strategy

The key insight from the examples:
- Example 1: n=3, m=1 at (2,2). Answer is 0.
- Example 2: n=3, m=0. Answer is 1.
- Example 3: n=4, m=3 at (3,1),(3,2),(3,3). Answer is 1.

From example 2: we can place at most 1 chip on a 3×3 grid.

From example 3: we can place at most 1 chip on a 4×4 grid with some banned cells.

I believe the maximum is either 0 or 1. Why?

Because of the symmetric nature of movement and the swap condition:
- If we place 2 chips, they will likely swap or collide due to the symmetry of movement.
- Each chip's path is determined by its starting position and direction.

Actually, let me think about the movement. In an n×n grid, after n-1 minutes, each chip reaches the opposite border. 

The real constraint is that chips moving in the same row or column will eventually meet or cross paths.

After analyzing the problem more deeply, I believe the maximum number of chips is at most 1. Why?

Because:
1. If we place 2 chips, they will have paths that are either parallel or intersecting.
2. When paths intersect, either they collide or they swap.
3. The swap condition specifically prohibits this.

Therefore, we can place at most 1 chip, AND only if that chip's path doesn't hit any banned cells.

Wait, let me check the first example: n=3, m=1 at (2,2). Answer is 0.

For n=3, border cells (not corners):
- Top: (1,2)
- Bottom: (3,2) 
- Left: (2,1)
- Right: (2,3)

For chip at (1,2): moves down to (2,2), then to (3,2)
For chip at (3,2): moves up to (2,2), then to (1,2)
For chip at (2,1): moves right to (2,2), then to (2,3)
For chip at (2,3): moves left to (2,2), then to (2,1)

If we place a chip at (1,2), it goes to (2,2) - which is banned, so we lose.
Similarly, (3,2) goes to (2,2) - banned.
(2,1) goes to (2,2) - banned.
(2,3) goes to (2,2) - banned.

So no valid position exists, hence answer is 0.

For n=3, m=0: any of the border cells work, so we can place 1 chip.

For n=4, with banned cells at (3,1),(3,2),(3,3): border cells are (1,2),(1,3),(4,2),(4,3),(2,1),(2,4),(3,1) is banned, (3,2) banned, (3,3) banned, (3,4) is banned? Wait, (3,4) is on right border.

Border cells (not corners) for n=4:
- Top: (1,2), (1,3)
- Bottom: (4,2), (4,3)
- Left: (2,1), (3,1)
- Right: (2,4), (3,4)

But (3,1), (3,2), (3,3) are banned, so (3,1) and (3,4) are banned.

So valid positions: (1,2), (1,3), (4,2), (4,3), (2,1), (2,4)

Now check paths:
- (1,2) → (2,2) → (3,2) → (4,2). But (3,2) is banned, so invalid.
- (1,3) → (2,3) → (3,3) → (4,3). But (3,3) is banned, so invalid.
- (4,2) → (3,2) → (2,2) → (1,2). (3,2) is banned, so invalid.
- (4,3) → (3,3) → (2,3) → (1,3). (3,3) is banned, so invalid.
- (2,1) → (3,1) → (4,1). (3,1) is banned, so invalid.
- (2,4) → (3,4) → (4,4). (3,4) is not banned? The banned cells are (3,1),(3,2),(3,3), so (3,4) is valid.

(2,4) → (3,4) → (4,4). No banned cells in path, so this position works.

So we can place 1 chip at (2,4).

Therefore, the answer is 1.

## Algorithm

1. Generate all valid border cells (not corners)
2. For each border cell, check if the path of moving toward opposite border hits any banned cell
3. If no path hits a banned cell, then we can place a chip there
4. The maximum number of chips is the number of such valid positions, but due to swapping constraints, we can place at most 1 chip.

Wait, is it really at most 1? The problem says we lose if two chips swap. 

But considering the movement patterns, if we place 2 chips, their paths might cross symmetrically causing a swap. 

Given that the examples all show answer 0 or 1, and the constraints are symmetric, I believe the maximum is the number of valid border positions without banned cells, but with the constraint that we can only place one chip due to swap condition.

Actually, re-reading: "at least once two chips swapped" - this happens when chips exchange positions.

For a chip at (1,2) and (4,2), they would move along the same column and never swap.

But if we have a chip at (1,2) and (4,2), both moving down/right/left, they might meet.

In the 3×3 case:
- (1,2) → (2,2) → (3,2)
- (3,2) → (2,2) → (1,2)

This is a swap! The chips exchange positions.

Therefore, if two chips are symmetric with respect to the center, they will swap.

So we cannot have two chips that are symmetric across the center.

However, we can have chips that are not symmetric.

But the answer in all examples is at most 1.

Looking at the examples again and the constraints, I believe the maximum number of chips is either 0 or 1.

So the solution is: count the number of valid starting positions (border not corners) where the path to the opposite border doesn't hit any banned cell.

```python
def solve():
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    banned = set()
    for _ in range(m):
        x, y = map(int, input().split())
        banned.add((x, y))
    
    # Border cells (not corners)
    valid_positions = []
    
    # Top edge (row 1)
    for j in range(2, n):  # columns 2 to n-1
        valid_positions.append((1, j))
    
    # Bottom edge (row n)
    for j in range(2, n):  # columns 2 to n-1
        valid_positions.append((n, j))
    
    # Left edge (column 1)
    for i in range(2, n):  # rows 2 to n-1
        valid_positions.append((i, 1))
    
    # Right edge (column n)
    for i in range(2, n):  # rows 2 to n-1
        valid_positions.append((i, n))
    
    # Check each position: can a chip start here without hitting banned cells?
    valid_count = 0
    
    for r, c in valid_positions:
        # Simulate the path of the chip
        # The chip will move from (r,c) to the opposite border
        # If r is 1 (top), it moves down
        # If r is n (bottom), it moves up
        # If c is 1 (left), it moves right
        # If c is n (right), it moves left
        
        if r == 1:  # Moving down
            # Path: (1,c), (2,c), (3,c), ..., (n,c)
            for i in range(1, n + 1):
                if i == 1:
                    current = (1, c)
                else:
                    current = (i, c)
                if current in banned:
                    break
            else:
                # No banned cell in path
                valid_count += 1
                
        elif r == n:  # Moving up
            # Path: (n,c), (n-1,c), ..., (1,c)
            for i in range(n, 0, -1):
                current = (i, c)
                if current in banned:
                    break
            else:
                valid_count += 1
                
        elif c == 1:  # Moving right
            # Path: (r,1), (r,2), ..., (r,n)
            for j in range(1, n + 1):
                current = (r, j)
                if current in banned:
                    break
            else:
                valid_count += 1
                
        elif c == n:  # Moving left
            # Path: (r,n), (r,n-1), ..., (r,1)
            for j in range(n, 0, -1):
                current = (r, j)
                if current in banned:
                    break
            else:
                valid_count += 1
    
    return valid_count

print(solve())
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Artem found a grasshopper. He brought it to his house and constructed a jumping area for him.

The area looks like a strip of cells 1 × n. Each cell contains the direction for the next jump and the length of that jump. Grasshopper starts in the first cell and follows the instructions written on the cells. Grasshopper stops immediately if it jumps out of the strip. Now Artem wants to find out if this will ever happen.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000) — length of the strip. 

Next line contains a string of length n which consists of characters "<" and ">" only, that provide the direction of the jump from the corresponding cell. Next line contains n integers d_{i} (1 ≤ d_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the length of the jump from the i-th cell.


-----Output-----

Print "INFINITE" (without quotes) if grasshopper will continue his jumps forever. Otherwise print "FINITE" (without quotes).


-----Examples-----
Input
2
><
1 2

Output
FINITE

Input
3
>><
2 1 1

Output
INFINITE


-----Note-----

In the first sample grasshopper starts from the first cell and jumps to the right on the next cell. When he is in the second cell he needs to jump two cells left so he will jump out of the strip.

Second sample grasshopper path is 1 - 3 - 2 - 3 - 2 - 3 and so on. The path is infinite.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of n cells, each with a direction ('<' or '>') and a jump length
2. Grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0)
3. From each cell i, it jumps according to the direction and length
4. If the jump takes it outside the strip [0, n-1], it stops
5. We need to determine if the grasshopper will jump forever (INFINITE) or eventually exit (FINITE)

## Key Insights

1. If the grasshopper jumps outside the strip, it stops → FINITE
2. If it gets into a cycle (visits the same cells repeatedly), it will jump forever → INFINITE
3. Since there are only n cells, if the grasshopper visits any cell twice, it's in a cycle
4. We need to simulate the jumps and track visited positions

## Algorithm

1. Start at position 0
2. Keep track of visited positions in a set
3. For each position:
   - Check if we've been here before → cycle detected → INFINITE
   - Check if the jump takes us outside [0, n-1] → FINITE
   - Move to the new position based on direction and jump length
4. If we exit the strip, return FINITE
5. If we detect a cycle, return INFINITE

## Step-by-step simulation

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1: n=2, directions="><", jumps=[1,2]**
- Start at position 0
- Direction '>': jump 1 right → position 1
- At position 1: direction '<': jump 2 left → position 1-2 = -1 (out of bounds)
- Stops → FINITE

**Example 2: n=3, directions=">><", jumps=[2,1,1]**
- Start at position 0
- Direction '>': jump 2 right → position 2
- At position 2: direction '<': jump 1 left → position 1  
- At position 1: direction '>' : jump 1 right → position 2
- We're back to position 2, which we've seen before → cycle → INFINITE

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
position = 0

while True:
    # If we've visited this position before, we're in a cycle
    if position in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        exit()
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(position)
    
    # Get current direction and jump length
    direction = directions[position]
    jump_length = jumps[position]
    
    # Calculate next position
    if direction == '>':
        next_position = position + jump_length
    else:  # direction == '<'
        next_position = position - jump_length
    
    # Check if next position is out of bounds
    if next_position < 0 or next_position >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        exit()
    
    # Move to next position
    position = next_position
```

The algorithm works by simulating each jump and checking:
1. If we've been to this position before → infinite loop
2. If the jump takes us outside the strip → finite
3. Otherwise, continue to the next position

Time complexity: O(n) in worst case (each cell visited at most once)
Space complexity: O(n) for storing visited positions

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Artem found a grasshopper. He brought it to his house and constructed a jumping area for him.

The area looks like a strip of cells 1 × n. Each cell contains the direction for the next jump and the length of that jump. Grasshopper starts in the first cell and follows the instructions written on the cells. Grasshopper stops immediately if it jumps out of the strip. -----Output-----

Print "INFINITE" (without quotes) if grasshopper will continue his jumps forever. Otherwise print "FINITE" (without quotes).

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of n cells, each containing a direction ('<' or '>') and a jump length
2. The grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0) and follows the instructions
3. It jumps according to the direction and length from each cell
4. We need to determine if the grasshopper will eventually jump out of the strip (FINITE) or loop forever (INFINITE)

## Key Insights

1. The grasshopper will jump out of the strip if it moves to an index < 0 or >= n
2. The grasshopper will have an infinite loop if it revisits a cell it has already been to
3. Since there are only n cells, if the grasshopper visits any cell twice, it will create a cycle

## Algorithm

1. Simulate the grasshopper's movement step by step
2. Keep track of all cells we've visited
3. If we jump out of bounds (index < 0 or index >= n), return "FINITE"
4. If we visit a cell we've seen before, we have a cycle → return "INFINITE"
5. Continue until one of these conditions is met

## Step-by-step Solution

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: n=2, directions="><", jumps=[1,2]
- Start at cell 0 (direction '>', jump length 1): position = 0 + 1 = 1
- At cell 1 (direction '<', jump length 2): position = 1 - 2 = -1 (out of bounds)
- Result: FINITE

Example 2: n=3, directions=">><", jumps=[2,1,1]
- Start at cell 0 (direction '>', jump length 2): position = 0 + 2 = 2
- At cell 2 (direction '<', jump length 1): position = 2 - 1 = 1
- At cell 1 (direction '>', jump length 1): position = 1 + 1 = 2
- We're back to cell 2, which we visited before → cycle → INFINITE

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
position = 0

while True:
    # If we're out of bounds, grasshopper stops
    if position < 0 or position >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
    
    # If we've visited this position before, we have a cycle
    if position in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(position)
    
    # Calculate next position based on direction and jump length
    if directions[position] == '>':
        position += jumps[position]
    else:  # directions[position] == '<'
        position -= jumps[position]
```

The algorithm works as follows:
1. Start at position 0
2. For each position, check if we're out of bounds (return FINITE)
3. Check if we've visited this position before (return INFINITE)
4. Mark current position as visited
5. Move to next position based on direction and jump length
6. Repeat until we find either an exit or a cycle

Time complexity: O(n) in the worst case since we can visit at most n unique cells.
Space complexity: O(n) for the visited set.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of `n` cells, each containing a direction (`<` or `>`) and a jump length
2. Grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0)
3. From each cell, it jumps according to the direction and length
4. If the jump takes it outside the strip (index < 0 or index >= n), it stops
5. We need to determine if the grasshopper will eventually jump out (FINITE) or jump in a cycle forever (INFINITE)

## Key Insights

1. The grasshopper will eventually stop if it jumps outside the strip
2. The grasshopper will go infinite if it enters a cycle (visits the same cells repeatedly)
3. We need to simulate the jumps and detect cycles
4. A cycle occurs when we visit the same cell twice in a row

## Algorithm

1. Start at position 0
2. Keep track of visited positions
3. For each step:
   - Calculate next position based on current direction and jump length
   - If next position is out of bounds (negative or >= n), return "FINITE"
   - If we've visited this position before, we have a cycle → return "INFINITE"
   - Otherwise, mark this position as visited and continue
4. If we exit the loop without finding a cycle or going out of bounds, return "FINITE"

## Step-by-step solution

1. Read inputs: n, direction string, jump lengths
2. Initialize current position and visited set
3. While the position is valid:
   - Calculate next position
   - Check if next position is out of bounds
   - Check if we've visited this position before
   - Update position and visited set
4. Return result

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
current_pos = 0

while True:
    # Check if current position is valid
    if current_pos < 0 or current_pos >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
    
    # If we've been here before, we have a cycle
    if current_pos in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(current_pos)
    
    # Calculate next position based on direction
    if directions[current_pos] == '>':
        next_pos = current_pos + jumps[current_pos]
    else:  # directions[current_pos] == '<'
        next_pos = current_pos - jumps[current_pos]
    
    # Update current position
    current_pos = next_pos
    
    # Check if next position is out of bounds
    if current_pos < 0 or current_pos >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
```

Wait, I need to be more careful about the logic. Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: n=2, directions="><", jumps=[1,2]
- Start at pos 0: direction '>' → jump 1 → next_pos = 0+1 = 1
- At pos 1: direction '<' → jump 2 → next_pos = 1-2 = -1 (out of bounds)
- Output: FINITE ✓

**Example 2**: n=3, directions=">><", jumps=[2,1,1]
- Start at pos 0: direction '>' → jump 2 → next_pos = 0+2 = 2
- At pos 2: direction '<' → jump 1 → next_pos = 2-1 = 1
- At pos 1: direction '>' → jump 1 → next_pos = 1+1 = 2
- We're at pos 2, which we've already visited → cycle detected → INFINITE ✓

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Artem found a grasshopper. He brought it to his house and constructed a jumping area for him.

The area looks like a strip of cells 1 × n. Each cell contains the direction for the next jump and the length of that jump. Grasshopper starts in the first cell and follows the instructions written on the cells. Grasshopper stops immediately if it jumps out of the strip. Now Artem wants to find out if this will ever happen.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000) — length of the strip. 

Next line contains a string of length n which consists of characters "<" and ">" only, that provide the direction of the jump from the corresponding cell. Next line contains n integers d_{i} (1 ≤ d_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the length of the jump from the i-th cell.


-----Output-----

Print "INFINITE" (without quotes) if grasshopper will continue his jumps forever. Otherwise print "FINITE" (without quotes).


-----Examples-----
Input
2
><
1 2

Output
FINITE

Input
3
>><
2 1 1

Output
INFINITE

In the first sample grasshopper starts from the first cell and jumps to the right on the next cell. When he is in the second cell he needs to jump two cells left so he will jump out of the strip.

Second sample grasshopper path is 1 - 3 - 2 - 3 - 2 - 3 and so on. The path is infinite.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of n cells, each with a direction ("<" or ">") and a jump length
2. Grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0)
3. From each cell, it jumps according to the direction and length
4. If the jump takes it outside the strip (index < 0 or index >= n), it stops
5. We need to determine if the grasshopper will jump forever (INFINITE) or eventually fall off (FINITE)

## Key Insights

1. If the grasshopper ever jumps outside the strip, the answer is FINITE
2. If the grasshopper enters a cycle (visits the same sequence of cells repeatedly), the answer is INFINITE
3. Since we have a finite number of cells (n), if we revisit any cell, we're in a cycle
4. We need to track visited cells to detect cycles

## Algorithm

1. Start at position 0 (first cell)
2. Keep track of visited positions in a set
3. For each step:
   - If we jump outside the strip → FINITE
   - If we visit a cell we've seen before → INFINITE (cycle detected)
   - Otherwise, mark current position as visited and jump to next position
4. Return the result

## Step-by-step Solution

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:** n=2, directions="><", jumps=[1,2]
- Start at position 0
- From position 0: direction '>' means jump right by 1 → position 0+1 = 1
- From position 1: direction '<' means jump left by 2 → position 1-2 = -1
- Position -1 is outside strip → FINITE

**Example 2:** n=3, directions=">><", jumps=[2,1,1]
- Start at position 0
- From position 0: direction '>' means jump right by 2 → position 0+2 = 2
- From position 2: direction '<' means jump left by 1 → position 2-1 = 1
- From position 1: direction '>' means jump right by 1 → position 1+1 = 2
- We're back to position 2, which we've seen before → INFINITE (cycle detected)

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
position = 0

while True:
    # If we're out of bounds, grasshopper stops
    if position < 0 or position >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
    
    # If we've visited this position before, we have a cycle
    if position in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(position)
    
    # Calculate next position based on direction and jump length
    if directions[position] == '>':
        position += jumps[position]
    else:  # directions[position] == '<'
        position -= jumps[position]
```

The algorithm works as follows:

1. We use a set to track visited positions
2. We simulate each jump step by step
3. At each step, we check if we've gone out of bounds (FINITE) or if we've visited this position before (INFINITE)
4. If neither happens, we continue to the next jump

Time complexity: O(n) in the worst case (we visit at most n different positions)
Space complexity: O(n) for storing visited positions

This solution correctly handles both examples and will detect infinite loops by identifying cycles in the grasshopper's path.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of n cells, each containing a direction (`<` or `>`) and a jump length
2. Grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0)
3. From each cell, it jumps according to the direction and length
4. If the grasshopper jumps outside the strip (index < 0 or index >= n), it stops
5. We need to determine if the grasshopper will jump forever (INFINITE) or eventually exit (FINITE)

## Key Insights

1. If the grasshopper ever jumps outside the strip, the answer is "FINITE"
2. If the grasshopper enters a cycle (visits the same cells repeatedly), the answer is "INFINITE"
3. The grasshopper stops only when it jumps outside the strip
4. We need to detect cycles in the path

## Algorithm Approach

1. Simulate the grasshopper's movement step by step
2. Keep track of all visited positions
3. If we visit a position we've seen before, we have a cycle → "INFINITE"
4. If we jump outside the strip, we stop → "FINITE"

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Read input: n, direction string, jump lengths
2. Start from position 0
3. Use a set to keep track of visited positions
4. While current position is within bounds:
   - Check if we've visited this position before (cycle detected)
   - If yes → return "INFINITE"
   - If no → mark as visited and make the next jump
5. If we exit the loop (jumped outside), return "FINITE"

## Edge Cases to Consider

- If n = 1: only one cell, jumps might go outside or cycle
- Jump lengths can be very large (up to 10^9), but we only care about positions within the strip

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
position = 0

while 0 <= position < n:
    # If we've been at this position before, we have a cycle
    if position in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        exit()
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(position)
    
    # Determine the next position based on direction and jump length
    if directions[position] == '>':
        position += jumps[position]
    else:  # directions[position] == '<'
        position -= jumps[position]

# If we exit the loop, we jumped outside the strip
print("FINITE")
```

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1:** n=2, directions="><", jumps=[1,2]
- Start at position 0
- position 0: direction '>' → position += 1 → position = 1
- position 1: direction '<' → position -= 2 → position = -1
- position = -1 < 0 → exit loop → "FINITE" ✓

**Example 2:** n=3, directions=">><", jumps=[2,1,1]
- Start at position 0
- position 0: direction '>' → position += 2 → position = 2
- position 2: direction '<' → position -= 1 → position = 1
- position 1: direction '>' → position += 1 → position = 2
- position 2: already visited → cycle detected → "INFINITE" ✓

The solution correctly handles both cases.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of n cells, each containing a direction ('<' or '>') and a jump length
2. The grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0)
3. From each cell, it jumps according to the direction and length
4. If the grasshopper jumps outside the strip (to index < 0 or >= n), it stops
5. We need to determine if the grasshopper will jump forever (INFINITE) or eventually exit (FINITE)

## Key Insights

1. The grasshopper continues forever if it enters a cycle - it keeps jumping between the same cells repeatedly
2. If it ever jumps outside the strip, it stops (FINITE)
3. We need to detect cycles in the jumping path

## Algorithm

1. Simulate the grasshopper's movement step by step
2. Keep track of which cells we've visited
3. If we visit a cell we've seen before, we have a cycle → INFINITE
4. If we jump outside the strip, we stop → FINITE

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input: n, direction string, jump lengths
2. Start at position 0
3. Use a set to track visited positions
4. While the current position is within bounds:
   - If we've visited this position before → cycle detected → INFINITE
   - Mark current position as visited
   - Calculate next position based on direction and jump length
   - Move to next position
5. If we exit the strip → FINITE

## Edge Cases to Consider

- Single cell: if direction is '<' or '>', it jumps out immediately
- Jump lengths can be very large (up to 10^9)
- The simulation might skip many cells, so we need to be careful about performance

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
current_pos = 0

while 0 <= current_pos < n:
    if current_pos in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        exit()
    
    visited.add(current_pos)
    
    # Calculate next position
    if directions[current_pos] == '>':
        next_pos = current_pos + jumps[current_pos]
    else:  # directions[current_pos] == '<'
        next_pos = current_pos - jumps[current_pos]
    
    current_pos = next_pos

print("FINITE")
```

## Explanation of the Solution

1. **Initialization**: We start at position 0, track visited positions in a set
2. **Loop**: While we're within bounds:
   - Check if we've visited this position before (cycle detected)
   - Mark current position as visited
   - Calculate next position based on direction and jump length
   - Update current position
3. **Termination**: If we exit the loop (jumped outside bounds), print "FINITE"

## Why This Works

- If the grasshopper visits the same cell twice, it means it's in a cycle and will continue forever
- If it jumps outside the strip, it stops and we return "FINITE"
- The cycle detection ensures we don't simulate infinitely

The time complexity is O(n) in the worst case since we visit each cell at most once, and the space complexity is O(n) for storing visited positions.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of n cells, each containing a direction (left '<' or right '>') and a jump length
2. Grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0)
3. From each cell, it jumps according to the direction and length
4. If the grasshopper jumps outside the strip (index < 0 or index >= n), it stops
5. We need to determine if the grasshopper will eventually jump out (FINITE) or jump forever in a cycle (INFINITE)

## Key Insight

The grasshopper will jump forever if it enters a cycle - it keeps visiting the same cells repeatedly. If it never enters a cycle and eventually jumps out, it's FINITE.

## Strategy

1. Simulate the grasshopper's path starting from cell 0
2. Keep track of visited positions to detect cycles
3. If we visit a position we've seen before, we have a cycle → INFINITE
4. If we jump outside the bounds, we stop → FINITE

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input: n, direction string, jump lengths
2. Start at position 0
3. Use a set to track visited positions
4. For each step:
   - Check if current position is out of bounds (already handled by the jump logic)
   - Calculate next position based on direction and jump length
   - If next position is out of bounds, return "FINITE"
   - If we've visited this position before, return "INFINITE"
   - Mark current position as visited and move to next position

## Edge Cases to Consider

- Starting at position 0
- Jump length can be very large (up to 10^9)
- The grasshopper might jump far and then come back

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
current_pos = 0

while True:
    # If we're already out of bounds, we stop
    if current_pos < 0 or current_pos >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
    
    # If we've visited this position before, we have a cycle
    if current_pos in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(current_pos)
    
    # Calculate next position based on direction
    if directions[current_pos] == '>':
        next_pos = current_pos + jumps[current_pos]
    else:  # directions[current_pos] == '<'
        next_pos = current_pos - jumps[current_pos]
    
    # Move to next position
    current_pos = next_pos

    if current_pos in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(current_pos)
    
    # Calculate next position
    if directions[current_pos] == '>':
        next_pos = current_pos + jumps[current_pos]
    else:  # directions[current_pos] == '<'
        next_pos = current_pos - jumps[current_pos]
    
    # Update current position for next iteration
    current_pos = next_pos
```

This looks correct. The grasshopper only stops when it jumps out of bounds (which happens when the next position is < 0 or >= n). If it visits a position twice, it's in a cycle (INFINITE). Otherwise, it eventually jumps out (FINITE).

The algorithm will terminate because:
- Either we find a cycle (INFINITE) 
- Or we eventually jump out of bounds (FINITE)

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

visited = set()
current_pos = 0

while True:
    # If current position is out of bounds, grasshopper stopped
    if current_pos < 0 or current_pos >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
    
    # If we've visited this position before, we have a cycle
    if current_pos in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(current_pos)
    
    # Calculate next position based on direction
    if directions[current_pos] == '>':
        current_pos += jumps[current_pos]
    else:  # directions[current_pos] == '<'
        current_pos -= jumps[current_pos]
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Little Artem found a grasshopper. He brought it to his house and constructed a jumping area for him.

The area looks like a strip of cells 1 × n. Each cell contains the direction for the next jump and the length of that jump. Grasshopper starts in the first cell and follows the instructions written on the cells. Grasshopper stops immediately if it jumps out of the strip. Now Artem wants to find out if this will ever happen.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100 000) — length of the strip. 

Next line contains a string of length n which consists of characters "<" and ">" only, that provide the direction of the jump from the corresponding cell. Next line contains n integers d_{i} (1 ≤ d_{i} ≤ 10^9) — the length of the jump from the i-th cell.


-----Output-----

Print "INFINITE" (without quotes) if grasshopper will continue his jumps forever. Otherwise print "FINITE" (without quotes).


-----Examples-----
Input
2
><
1 2

Output
FINITE

Input
3
>><
2 1 1

Output
INFINITE


-----Note-----

In the first sample grasshopper starts from the first cell and jumps to the right on the next cell. When he is in the second cell he needs to jump two cells left so he will jump out of the strip.

Second sample grasshopper path is 1 - 3 - 2 - 3 - 2 - 3 and so on. The path is infinite.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a strip of `n` cells, each containing a direction (`<` or `>`) and a jump length
2. The grasshopper starts at cell 1 (index 0)
3. From each cell, it jumps according to the direction and length
4. If the grasshopper jumps outside the strip (index < 0 or index >= n), it stops
5. We need to determine if the grasshopper will eventually jump out (FINITE) or keep jumping forever (INFINITE)

## Key Insights

1. If the grasshopper jumps out of bounds, the answer is "FINITE"
2. If the grasshopper gets into a cycle (visits the same cell multiple times), the answer is "INFINITE"
3. We need to simulate the path and detect cycles

## Algorithm Approach

1. Start at position 0
2. Keep track of visited positions
3. For each step:
   - Calculate next position based on current position, direction, and jump length
   - If next position is out of bounds (negative or >= n), return "FINITE"
   - If we've visited this position before, we have a cycle → return "INFINITE"
   - Otherwise, mark this position as visited and continue

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input: n, direction string, jump lengths
2. Initialize current position and visited set
3. Simulate jumps until we either go out of bounds or detect a cycle
4. Return appropriate result

```python
n = int(input())
directions = input().strip()
jumps = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Keep track of visited positions to detect cycles
visited = set()
current_pos = 0

while True:
    # Check if we're out of bounds
    if current_pos < 0 or current_pos >= n:
        print("FINITE")
        break
    
    # If we've been here before, we have a cycle
    if current_pos in visited:
        print("INFINITE")
        break
    
    # Mark current position as visited
    visited.add(current_pos)
    
    # Determine next position based on direction
    if directions[current_pos] == '>':
        current_pos += jumps[current_pos]
    else:  # directions[current_pos] == '<'
        current_pos -= jumps[current_pos]
```

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1:**
- n=2, directions="><", jumps=[1,2]
- Start at position 0: direction '>' → jump 1 → position 1
- At position 1: direction '<' → jump 2 → position 1-2 = -1 (out of bounds)
- Output: "FINITE" ✓

**Example 2:**
- n=3, directions=">><", jumps=[2,1,1]
- Start at position 0: direction '>' → jump 2 → position 2
- At position 2: direction '<' → jump 1 → position 1
- At position 1: direction '>' → jump 1 → position 2
- At position 2: direction '<' → jump 1 → position 1
- We've seen position 1 before → cycle detected → "INFINITE" ✓

The solution correctly handles both cases.

- Input: "crc" followed by "1"
- Output: "crc"

The pattern shows that we're taking the first part of the input (before the number) and outputting it unchanged.

## Step-by-Step Analysis

1. **Input format**: The input consists of a string followed by a number
2. **Output format**: The output is just the string part, not including the number
3. **Pattern**: The problem appears to be extracting the string portion before the number

## Solution Approach

1. Read the entire input from stdin
2. Find the position where the number starts (after the string)
3. Extract the string portion before the number
4. Output the extracted string

## Implementation

```python
# Read input from stdin
line = input().strip()

# Find the first digit in the input
first_digit_index = -1
for i, char in enumerate(line):
    if char.isdigit():
        first_digit_index = i
        break

# If we found a digit, extract the part before it
if first_digit_index != -1:
    result = line[:first_digit_index]
else:
    # If no digit is found, return the entire string
    result = line

# Print the result
print(result)
```

## Verification with Example

- Input: "crc1"
- Iterating through characters: 'c' (not digit), 'r' (not digit), 'c' (not digit), '1' (digit)
- first_digit_index = 3
- result = line[:3] = "crc"
- Output: "crc" ✓

This matches the expected output. The solution correctly extracts the string portion before the first digit.
